*Onm'Madhuva'ta rta'yate madhuks'arantu ...* "O gracious Brahma, may we be able to perceive thy all prevailing manifest in this infant in whose form thou has appeared in our midst today."

"We have unitedly given this baby the name........"

Thereafter, the guardian of the infant shall bathe it with that very holy water. Then the child will eat solid food for the first time. In this ceremony, the arrangement of a social feast depends entirely on the desire and the financial position of the persons concerned. Arranging a social feast by taking a loan or incurring a debt is strictly forbidden. Twenty one days after the birth of a child, the child and its mother will be considered clean from a worldly standpoint.

**Diiksa' Prana'lii (The process of initiation)** - When a child attains the age of five, its parent, brother, sister or any other guardian can initiate into "Na'ma mantra". I.e. when the child develops some knowledge in five years old, he/she should be taught to sit in the posture of *Padm'sana*. Instead of inserting the fingers of one hand into the inter-phalangeal spaces of the other, he/she may rest the dorsum of one hand over the palm of the other, keeping the vertebral column erect, and then to feel that everything around him/her and whatever he/she sees in *Brahma*.

**Relationship with the Acarya** - In the absence of any previous relationship and depending on their respective ages, the relationship between the *Acarya* and his "*Shiks'a'bra'ta* will be that of an elder and younger brother. In case the *Shiks'a'bhra'ta* is younger in age, he/she may greet the *Acarya* by touching his/her feet. Even if the *Acarya* be younger in age, the *Shiks'a'bra'ta*, if

he/she wishes, can touch the Acarya feet, but he/she would not lie prostrate. This rule is applicable to Gurubhra'ta also. The Acarya should always be addressed in respectful language.

**Laying of the foundation stone** - After a collective meditation, the main person will recite while laying the stone or the brick of a new house, "Ayamarambha Shubhaya Bhavatu".

Others present in the ceremony will utter:

> "May today's laying of stone be meaningful from all points of view. May this stone be a source of bliss to the neighbors, and may they be a source of bliss to this stone. May we speedily construct the house on this stone? *Oum Shanti, Oum Shanti, Oum Shanti.*"

**Grha Pravesh (First entrance into the House)** - After decorating the house with leaves, flowers, jugs, etc. the mistress shall first enter it at dawn followed by the other women of the family. Initiated persons shall while mentally reciting their Guru mantras. Thereafter, while they blow the conch shell, the male members will enter the house, accompanied by invited male and female guests.

Next, everyone present shall recite in chorus following the Acarya or the oldest person:

> *Oum Madhuva'ta rta'ynte...*
>
> "May today's entrance in the house be advantageous in every respect? May the neighbors prove sweet and pleasing to the occupants of the house? May the occupants of the house prove sweet and pleasing to the neighbors? May every occupant prove beneficial to the house, and may the house prove beneficial to its occupants. May we be able to protect and enlarge this house according to our capacity? May this house give us a peaceful shelter? Brahma *Madhu*, *Brahma Madhu*, Brahma *Madhu*".

**Vrks'a Ropana (Planting a tree)** - Ananda Marga advocates Neo-Humanism, which not only thinks of the welfare of human beings, but also of plants and animals. They are also living organisms, members of the cosmic system of existence and as such, have an equal right to live and develop like every other being.

*Ananda Marga* advises, "Plant more and more trees." While watering the tree, jointly say (with the Acarya or an older person leading):

> *Ohm Madhuva'ta rta'yat...*
>
> "May the tree planted today prove felicitous for us with its fruits, flowers, fragrance, sweetness, leaves and shade? May we ourselves prove helpful to it through service, manure, water, and sunshine?"
>
> *Oum Shanti, oum Shanti, Oum Shanti....*

You shall devotedly and carefully tend the more beneficial trees, like *Tulasi* (basil), *Niima* (Margosa), *Ashoka*, Eucalyptus and also those bearing fruits and shade.

**Ya'tra Prakaran'a (Starting on a Journey)** - You shall not take into account the date and stars while setting out on your journey. You may, as necessary, set out after ascribing the feelings of Brahma in your journey through *Guru Mantra*. Depending on dates, stars etc necessitates the keeping of *Panjika* (almanac) all the time, this opposes practical sense.

**The Marriage System** - The marriage *pandal* (premises) and the house are to be decorated according to the economic status and taste of the bridegroom. If possible, the orchestra and music can enliven the marriage courtyard. The *Pratiika* (emblem) should be placed in an elevated place in the marriage premises. Incense may be used at the time of the marriage. At least ten persons shall be present. The bride and bridegroom should enter the marriage hall in clean dress and will take seats facing each other. The persons present shall greet them by blowing the conch or by any other auspicious sound. As directed by the Acarya, collective *Iishavara Pran'idhana* will be performed.

The priest's job in the marriage will be performed by two Acaryas (one on the groom's side and the other on the bride's side), for want of two, even one *Acarya*, and in the absence of

another, the most senior *Margii* present will function. Marriage of solemnization:

First the Acarya will recite: *Oum'madhuva'ta rta'yate...*

Following the *Acarya*, the bridegroom will repeat, "I say on oath, in the name of *Parama Brahma* and *Marga Gurudeva*, that I accept *Shriimatii*..... As my wife of my own free will. Henceforward, I take upon myself all the responsibility for her food, clothes, education, medical care, and the like."

Thereupon, the *Acarya* will recite: *Oum'madhuva'ta rta'yate...*

Then the bride shall say, "I say on oath in the name of *Parama Brahma* and *Marga Gurudeva* that I accept *Shrii*.... As my husband of my own free will. I take upon myself all the duties connected with this mundane life."

Then the Acarya will recite: *Oum'Madhuva'ta rta'yate.....*

Then the bridegroom shall say, "I say on oath in the name of *Parama Brahma* and *Marga Gurudeva* that I accept *Shriimatii*.... As my wife I shall be vigilant in every way for her spiritual progress."

Then the Acarya will recite: *Oum'Madhuva't rta'yate....*

Then the bride will say, "I say on oath in the name of *Parama Brahma* and *Marga Gurudeva* that I have accepted *Shrii*....... as my husband. I shall be vigilant in every way for his spiritual progress."

Then those present shall say, led by the *Acarya*, "We say on oath in the names of *Parama Brahma* and *Marga Gurudeva*, that we are the witnesses of this wedding. By the grace of merciful *Brahma*, may we be helpful, to the best of our capacity, for the all-round advancement of this newly married couple."

Thereafter, the newly wedded couple shall garland each other and exchange the garland three times. In the absence of garlands, flowers may be exchanged. If he so desires the bridegroom can put vermilion in the parting of the hairs of the bride three times. If she so desires, the bride can touch the vermilion on the forehead of the bridegroom. The newly weded couple will hold each other's hands. The conch can be blown and other auspicious sounds made. If possible, arrangements can be made for vocal and instrumental music. The newly wedded couple will salute the *Acaryas* and the guardians.

The marriage feast is completely optional, it depends on the financial capacity of the persons concerned. Arranging the feast by taking a loan or incurring debt is prohibited.

**A few instructions:**

1. Guardians should not consider the caste and nationality in settling marriages, but they must consider the family and merits and demerits of the bride and bridegroom. The guardians, before fixing the marriage and proceeding further, shall seek the opinions of the bride and bridegroom. Where marriages are decided upon by guardians, they should not allow matrimony between persons connected with each other either on paternal or maternal side, for three generations, ascending or descending.

2. The ceremony of finally seeing (and blessing) should be completed at least a day before the marriage and it should be specifically ascertained that the bride and bridegroom are not unwilling.

3. If the boy and girl settle their marriage themselves, it is proper for the guardians to give their consent. In case the guardians feel that the consequences of such a marriage may be harmful, they will ask the boy and girl to reconsider the matter. Even then, if they do not revise their opinion, the guardians will give their consent, but they will in no way be responsible for such a marriage.

4. Every person should marry if there is not sufficient reason against marriage. A decision for marriage should be taken with full consideration of one's physical, mental, and financial condition and environmental circumstance. No one should exert pressure in the matter of marriage. Marriage is not a hindrance to *Dharma Sa'dhana*, marriage is *Dharmika* ceremony.

5. Male members of the Marga can marry females outside the Marga, but it would be more suitable for the male/female to marry a female/male belonging to the Marga. If a suitable match is available outside the Marga, the marriage may be solemnized, but efforts should be made to initiate the spouse into the Marga without delay.

6. For marriage, neither party can demand a dowry.

7. A widow or a woman forsaken by her husband can remarry. A man marrying such a woman should be given a special status in the society. He will have to shoulder the responsibility of bringing up the children of her previous husband.

8. If a woman forsaken by the society desires to lead an honorable life, proper opportunities should be afforded her. If anyone marries such a woman in the manner prescribed by the *Marga*, the marriage should be properly respected.

9. Prove your manliness by marrying a shelterless woman. Do not at all permit her to lead a shameful life.

10. It is proper not to marry again when one's wife is living. But sometimes more than one marriage can be accepted due to social or family necessity. If there is need for more than one marriage, the clear permission of the wife has to be taken in the presence of five responsible persons (one of whom should preferable be an *Acarya*). The second marriage will not be permissible without the permission of the first wife. These five persons will specifically ascertain the truth in the statement of the petitioner.

11. In *Ananda Marga*, no one will be looked down upon as an illegitimate child. In such an eventuality, the parents of the child will be compelled to marry in the prescribed manner, and, if need be in such a circumstance, the male member will have to agree to more than one marriage. In order to save the prestige of the illegitimate child, permission from the earlier wife before the marriage will not be necessary.

12. The mantras etc. in the *Ananda Marga* marriage are such that the question of divorce does not arise, but in very extraordinary circumstances in charges of characterlessness, irresponsibility, or cruelty, divorce may be accepted. The complainant (man or woman) will appeal to five important responsible persons of the Marga, An *Acarya* preferably being one of them. They on being satisfied regarding the validity of the complaints will allow the complainant six months' time for reconsideration. If the petition is still not withdrawn and the reasons for the complaint remain unaltered, divorce will be accepted. In this connection, the procedure for the division of the properties shall be formulated according to the demand at the time.

Birthday ceremony - The person celebrating his or her birthday should take presentations and food after *Iishavara Pranidhana* and will receive blessings and the auspicious *Tilaka* (a mark made with the fingers in between the eyebrows with sandal paste, vermilion etc.) from the elders and accept salutations, garlands and sandal paste from the juniors. Incense, candles, conch shell etc are not indispensable for any auspicious function, but these can be used.

Social functions and festivals - Make arrangements for different types of delightful festivities on the occasion of the ceremonial functions of the Marga, but see that those who may attain in some way healthy opportunities for their physical, mental, and spiritual growth. Among the social functions:

1. *Ananda Purnima* - *Vaeshakhii Purnima*
2. *Shravanii Purnima*
3. *Sharadotsava* - from the sixth to the tenth day of the bright fortnight in the months of Ashvina
4. *Diipavalii* - *Kartikii Amavasya*
5. *Bhratrdvittiya* - the second day of the fortnight in the month of kartika
6. *Navanna* - This will celebrated on any *Purnima*

(Full moon) of the season in which the main crop of the particular country is harvested.

7. New Year's Day - The first day of the International Calendar (Presently January 1st and the first day of the local calendar).
8. *Vasantotsava* - *Phalgunii Purnima*

**Details of the Functions:**

1. *Ananda Purnima* - Collective bath with Snana Mantra, Collective *Iishvara Pranidhana*, collective noon and night meals, merry celebrations, spiritual discourses, annual meetings of workers, and children's sports.
2. *Shravanii Purnima* - Twice collective *Iishvara Pranidhana* and *Varnarghyadana*, collective noon and night meals, merry celebrations, spiritual discourses, literary meetings and processions.
3. *Sharadotsava* -
   a. *Sashtii* (Children's Day) - collective *Iishvara Pranidhana* and *Varnarghyadana*, merry celebrations, baby show, children's sports, exhibition and collective feast.
   b. *Saptamii* (Public day) - (for all those who are not children) collective *Iishvara Pranidhana* and *Varnarghyadana*, merry celebrations, youth health exhibition, exhibition of strength and adult sports.
   c. *Astamii* (fine arts day) - collective *Iishvara pranidhana* and *varnarghyadana* once, merry celebrations, literary meetings and various types of fine arts exhibition.
   d. *Navamii* (Music Day) - collective *Iishavara Pranidhana* and *Varnarghyadana* once, merry celebrations, music, instrumental music, and dance competition.
   e. *Dashami* (*Vijayotsava*) - procession with music wearing gay dress, then collective *Iishavara Pranidhana* and *Varnarghadana*, salutations and affectionate embraces, etc and entertaining the guests and visitors at one's residence.
4. *Deepavalii* - Collective *Iishvara Pranidhana* and *Varnarghyadana*, one illumination, collective merry celebrations, entertaining visitors at one's residence.
5. *Bhratrdvitiiya* - the brother shall receive the blessings and auspicious *Tilaka* of the elder sister and salutation and garland and sandal paste *Tilaka* from the younger sister. Mantra *"Bhrata meva cirayurbhavatu."*
6. *Navanna* - to perform *Iishavara Pranidhana* with and to feed one invited guest. Collective merry celebrations are the chief feature of this function.
7. New Year's Day - Collective *Iishvara Pranidhana* and *Varnarghyadana* twice. Collective merry celebrations. Sports and games for all, collective noon and night meals.
8. *Vasantotsava* - To play with colors and flowers amongst people of the same age group in the forenoon of the full moon. Youngest will offer these at the feet of the elders and the *Shiksabhrata* at the feet of his *Acarya*. The elders will not give these to their youngers. Then in the afternoon collective *Iishvara Pranidhana* and *Varnarghadana* (colored powder and flowers of one's own choice). In the end, to play with these among each other without having distinction of young or old.

# VII

## THE LORD SADASHIVA

From the first expression of moralism, to the establishment in cosmic humanity, there is a gap. The concerted effort to negotiate this gap is termed as social progress, and the collective body of those who are engaged in this concerted effort I shall call the "society."

![Lord Shiva](Lord Shiva)

Lord Shiva

## A Brief Autobiography of The Lord Shiva

According to the Indian mythology and history, Lord Shiva has been considered an Adiguru or immortal person. However, my guru Shrii Shrii *Anandamurtijii* has addressed him in the form of a very great entity. In His book, *Namah Shivah Shantaydh*, Baba has expressed Shiva as a great human being. On one occasion in the course of a field walk in Himachal Pradesh, He said that this district which is called Mahasu is really named after the great Shiva and the fact that Shiva was born in the vicinity of that area. Baba also said that Shiva was the offspring of a Tibetan lady and an Indian male. At the time that Shiva lived, the practice of marriage was not as prevalent. As a result no one had spoken or written about the wedding of Shiva's parents. Additionally, no one had ever asked Baba as to whom *Shiva's* parents were. Had they asked Baba this question, no doubt they would have received an answer. Thus, Shiva was not an ancient God, but a superhuman and in fact, the founder of Tantra and possessor of in-depth knowledge of the art of Tantra. This has been the history of Shiva and nobody had documented either the birth or the death or the various educative and history of Shiva's life. This is why in the Indian Puranas, there has been no mention of the start or end of Shiva's life.

My master speaks about Sada Shiva - The period of *Shiva* was a most turbulent period in India. On the one hand, there were the Aryans, the outsiders, and on the other hand, there were the indigenous people, with their Tantra-oriented culture and religion. Into this conflict-ridden environment, *Shiva* was born.

Now, what is the derivative meaning of Shiva? From the extant texts on *Tantra* and *Veda*, and from all other written and

unwritten sources, we get three meanings of the term *Shiva*. The first and most important meaning of Shiva is "Welfare":

> *"Anadianantamakhilasya madhye*
> *Vishvasya Srastraramanekarupam,*
> *Vishavasyaekam parivestram*
> *Jnatva Shivam Shantimatyantameei"*

Knowing that Shiva, who has neither beginning nor end, who is the creator of this vast universe; that multi-formed single entity who encompasses the whole universe, one attains eternal peace.

Here *Shiva* means "Welfare". *Shivamastu* means the same as *Kalyanmastu* - "May you be blessed". *Kalyanmastu* is the representation of Shiva embodying the true spirit of blessedness.

People say that He has been serving people, doing good to them, with five faces. He is described as having five faces: two on the left—*Vamadeva* and *Kalagni*, two on the right—*Dakineshvara* and *Ishana*, and one in the middle—*Kalyanasundara*, the Supreme Controller, which controls the entire individual's desires for action. *Dakshinehvara*, the extreme right face of *Shiva*, is so called because He is showering *Daksina* (compassion) upon the created beings. That is the special role allotted to *Dakshinehvara*. *Iishana* - the face next to the right—is responsible for controlling all the Jiivas, individual beings, with meticulous care, and *Kalayansundaram*, the face in the center, plays the role of controlling all the faces. Now the question is, why is *kalyanasundaram* assigned such a role? His only purpose is to promote the greatest welfare of all living beings, He has no other purpose.

In addition, Shiva has two faces on the left: *Vamadeva* is terrible—*Rudra, rudratirudra, rudro'pirudrah* - "one who teaches others by making them shed tears." But the underlying purpose is to teach people, not to harm them. The other face, *Kalagni*, also subjects people to excruciating torture, but there also, the main purpose is to teach them to promote their welfare.

Now here also, the two roles of *Vamadeva* and *Kalagni* are controlled by the central face, *Kalyansundaram*. He is *Sundram*, beautiful, because He promotes *Kalyana* (Welfare) hence, *"Kalyansundaram"*. He is terrible, but at the same time excellently calm and tranquil. Yet behind His apparent dreadfulness and tranquility lies the *kalyansundaram Bhava* (mental flow). He is both severe and tender, so naturally people love him. Although He is severe, people still adore him, because underlying His apparent severity is tenderness. Thus, the role of *Shiva* is predominantly the role of a promoter of welfare, so the first meaning of the term Shiva is "Welfare".

The second meaning of the term *Shiva* is "cognition in its zenith status"—the zenith status of the cognitive principle, the Supreme Non-Attributional Process, the Supreme Non-Attributional Entity beyond the faculties of all existential bondage.

The third meaning of *Sadashiva*, who was born into this world about seven thousand years ago—and who, by His holy birth, consecrated as it were, each and every dust particle of this earth and utilized His whole life for the sole purpose of advancing the cause of universal welfare. Remember, I have not said "Human welfare" because in our world, not only are there humans, there are also birds and animals, trees and plants. Shiva belongs to all, and for all living beings, He gave His all. Hence the people called him *"Sadashiva"*—*sada* means "always", and *Shiva*—as mentioned earlier—means "welfare. So *Sadashiva* means one whose only vow of existence is to promote the all-round welfare of all living beings".

Now the question remains: in the night of blinding darkness, do humans alone aspire to feel the soothing touch of light? No, all want it. All seek to grow out of the oblivion of existential darkness into the warmth of life, to experience finally the fulfillment of their life's urges. Up until this day, humans have not made a proper appraisal of this great personality, this

*Mahasambhuti*, who gave human beings their first opportunity to experience the sweet joy of fulfillment of all their longings. No one has discussed him much up until now.

Why people failed to make this appraisal is irrelevant today. It is the firm duty of every individual to know and evaluate the exact contribution of Shiva, and in this process of evaluation, we cannot ignore the personality himself. One may derive some joy from a bright ray emanating from a shining entity, but without the entity itself, the bliss will not be complete.

### LORD SHIVA (Severe and Tender Both)

Humans came into earth about one million years ago. But Human civilization is at most fifteen thousand years old. If the first Mandala (chapter) of the ancient Rgveda is accepted as the starting point of civilization, then the age of civilization will not exceed fifteen thousand years. But the civilization of those ancient days was not like the one you see in our times; the humans in those days merely followed a few rules and regulations in certain areas - not more than that.

Shiva was born about seven thousand years ago - about eight thousand years after the beginning of the composition of the Rgveda, that is, during the last part of the Rgvedic Age and the first part of the *Yajurvedic* Age. The civilization we see in the Rgveda may be considered as pre-Shiva, and the civilization we find in the days of the *Yajurveda* as post-*Shiva*. From what we observe in the early and mediaeval periods of the Rgveda, we can conclude that a well-regulated social order was not yet evolved. Social life was not at all systematized. The matriarchal social system was in decline, giving way to the patriarchal system. Under the patriarchal social system, the patriarchal order and the patriarchal system function side by side. But in those days, no fixed traditional had evolved. For instance, the matrilineal order persisted until the days of Buddha and Mahaviir Jain about 2500 years ago. At the time of Shiva, the matriarchal system was not very popular, but the matrilineal order was in full force. The existence of the patriarchal system naturally presupposes the existence of the patrilineal order, and to evolve a patrilineal order, the fathers must be identified in the society.

This is how the patrilineal order evolved: each community used to live on a hill, and that hill was named after the headman, or the patriarch, of the hill. The days of the matriarch were already past, and in those days, the patriarchs were the heads of the groups. In *Sanskrit*, a hill is called a *Gotra*. A particular community of people used to live on a hill, and particular *Rsi* (sage) used to be the acknowledged father or leader of that community, just as the *Gotramata*, the clan mother, had been in the matriarchal age. For instance, there was an *Rsi* named *Maharsi Kashyap* living on one hill, naturally that hill was named after him. Another *Rsi*, say *Maharsi Bhardvaja*, might be living living on another hill, that hill would be named after him. This is how the system of determining one's *Gotra* (clan) evolved. In each case, particular sage or patriarch was the head of a *gotra*; this is how the patriarchal system functioned. The matriarchal system had already ended, but the matrilineal order persisted after that, until the time of *Buddha*.

(If you want to know more about *Shiva*, see *"Namah Shivaya Shantaya"*).

### Contributions of Lord Shiva:

**1. The Great Unity**- *Shiva* did not give any particular importance to the matrilineal, matriarchal, patrilineal or patriarchal systems. He simply allowed people to grow in a natural way. But when it was necessary to maintain the patrilineal order, one's *Gotra* had to be ascertained. Shiva advised His followers; don't create conflicts among themselves in the name of *Gotras*. Previously there had been frequent conflicts between clans living on different hills. *Shiva* banned those conflicts and told people, those of you who love me, who obey me, should

accept the benevolent rule of a code of discipline. You should proclaim to the world, Henceforth, we shall no longer remain divided into various Gotras, and erect artificial walls of separation among ourselves. Rather we shall smash all these barriers. We shall all sit and eat and drink together in the same compound, bound by the ties of one human life.

In the social sphere, *Shiva* played a very active role in removing the distinctions amongst members of society. *Shiva* had three wives: *Parvati*, a fair-complexioned *Aryan* girl; *Kali*, a dark-complexioned non-*Aryan* girl; and *Ganga*, a yellow-complexioned *Mongolian* girl. *Shiva* wanted to unite the people by obliterating social differences.

2. Marriage system – Prior to *Shiva*, there was no system of marriage in human society. There was only a matrilineal order because their mothers could easily identify children. In the case of fathers, it was impossible to identify whose child was whose. *Shiva*, for the first time, introduced the system of marriage, which has continued until today, *Sanskrit* word for marriage means *Vivaha* (derived from *vi-vaha + ghain*). Literally, it means "to follow a particular system". This system of marriage is known as *Shaeva Vivaha*. According to this system, the bride and bridegroom are equally to share responsibility for their marriage, without consideration of caste or community. *Shiva* was above all sorts of geo-sentiment and socio-sentiment.

3. Medical Treatment – Lord *Sada Shiva* developed the *vaidyaka shastra*, a system of medicine based on surgery and dissection. This contribution of Lord Shiva was epoch-making in medical history. Thus, one of His names was *Vaedyanath* (Lord of physicians). Because He introduced both theoretical and practical procedures into medicine, *Shiva* continues to be regarded as an epochal personality.

4. Music – Based on the sound of the seven creatures, *Shiva* evolved the seven music notes. This musical *Sura saptaka*, which made the rhythm sweeter and more graceful, includes the sounds of the peacock (*Saraja*), ox (*Rsabha*), goat (*Gandhara*), horse (*Madhyama*), cuckoo (*Paincama*), ass (*Dhaevata*), and elephant (*Nisada*). By taking the initials of the seven animals—sa from *Saraja*, re from *Rsabha*, ga from *Gandhara*, ma from *Madhyama*, pa from *Paincama* and dha from *Dhaevata* and ni from *Nisada*, *Shiva* made the musical octave—*sa-re-ga-ma-pa-dha-ni-sa*. At the eighth step, the first sound of the octave is repeated, although in a different scale.

The music of the present day is entirely based on these seven notes. It is a matter of great regret that people have forgotten this science of music, whose foundation was laid by the intense efforts of Lord *Shiva*.

5. Dance – *Shiva* did not give a systematic form of music only—to this day people also utilize the fruits of His research in the field of phonetics. Phonetics depends on the science of breath, or inhalation and exhalation. On the basis of this science, *Shiva* invested the world of rhythm with *Mudra* (*Mudras* are subtle, symbolic gestures characteristic of oriental dance). He established harmony between rhythm and dance and added *Mudra* to them.

The mind has two main functions: to think and to remember. Before the time of *Shiva*, there was no exercise for developing these two faculties. Lord Shiva introduced an unprecedented dance called *"Tandava"* that benefits the body and mind and elevates the soul. These together earned him the name of *Nataraj* (lord of dance).

6. Dharma – *Dharma* means "innate characteristic". The essential or innate characteristic of human beings is the urge to obtain the supreme through morality and spiritual practice, *Shiva's* greatest contribution to humanity was in the concept of *Dharma*. People of His time were in some ways not far above animal mentality. They had no systematic understanding of spirituality. The Vedas also did not propound any systematic *dharma*. Of course, *Tantra* did exist before *Shiva*, made *Tantra*

systematic and regulated. He brought about a harmonious synthesis between those scattered *Tantras* and the people's spiritual urges for supreme fulfillment. The *Shiva Tantra* inspired people to move towards the supreme truth on the one hand, and on the other hand, it exhorted people, "Do not neglect the practical world; maintain a congenial adjustment with the external world". His instructions were "*vartamanesu vartate nityam*" (live in the present). Try to penetrate as deeply in your mind as you can and keep moving- *caraeveti, caraeveti* proceed on, proceed on. But do not forget the realities of the external world, because if you ignore the external realities, your internal peace will also be disturbed.

# VIII

## THE LORD KRISNA

"Whatever you have taken, it's from Him, the almighty. Whatever you rendered, it was rendered to Him. You came empty handed and you will go the same way. Whatever is yours today, it was somebody else's yesterday, and will be somebody else, day after tomorrow. You are delighted in feeling it is your own. Alas, this happiness is the root cause of your agonies."

![Lord Krisna](illustration)

Lord Krisna

### A Brief Autobiography of The Lord Krisna

*Krisna's* birth occurred in the midst of the night, on a new moon's dark night in the midst of *Kansa's* warehouse on a very stormy and rainy day. *Krisna's* mother was *Devaki*—she gave birth surrounded by very tight security. He being the creator, the choice of whom to keep wake and who to put to sleep was His, as it happened all the security guards fell asleep, the security gates magically opened and an unknown traveler in the form of a dog guided *Vasudeva* in the pitch-black night with the baby child. The flow of the river *yamuna* reduced tremendously, and the high level subsided miraculously to allow *Vasudeva* to cross the river and reach the borders of *Nandagram*. *Lokamaya* had already taken birth by *Yashoda*. *Vasudeva* swapped the two babies, leaving *Krisna* with *Yashoda* and carrying *Lokmaya* back the same treacherous route that He had come and re-entered the prison walls of *Kansa*. The doors closed back, the child started to wail and the crying of the baby girl woke the security guards who as per instructions immediately informed *Kansa* of the birth of the new baby.

*Krisna's* childhood was spent in *Gokul* along with His brother *Balaram*. It's said that *Devaki* had transplanted her womb in *Rohini's* womb. This was to be her seventh month. Current science talk about test-tube babies, but about 3000 years ago there is written documentation in the *Mahabharata* of transplantation of wombs from one mother to another.

*Krisna's* childhood was spent in the forests of *Gokul* and *Brindavan* and on the banks of the river *yamuna*. *Krisna's* parents are *Vasudeva* and *Devaki*, however it is contended that *Yashoda* was in many ways the real mother to *Krisna* since He spent His

childhood years with her. Krisna had a third mother as well, that being Rohini who was the natural mother to Balaram and *Subhadra*.

*Subhadra* is known in history as Krisna's sister, *Arjuna's* wife and the mother of *Abhimanyu*. Krisna spent His childhood with the cowherds in the jungles of Gokul and Brindavan. Through the offices of his uncle he staged a combat against the milk and cream tax that was taken from the cowherds and poor farmers. He terminated this process of collection of milk, cream and yogurt from the farmers as part of taxes being levied by the King. It is said that the King had a well dug out in which he would collect this milk from taxes, he would then proceed to bathe and cleanse himself in that milk, letting it run to waste. Krisna rebelled against this horrible unfair practice and at the age of 17, he killed Kansa.

In the town of *Ujjain* under the teaching of Guru *Vaishampan*, he received education. Due to the fact that the town of *Mathura* was being attacked incessantly, Krisna left and went to Dvarika.

Krisna's entire life was spent in conflict. On the battlefield of *Kuruksetra*, He gave a sermon in the form of the Holy Giita to *Arjuna*, which is now the topic of study and intellectual debate the world over. In current-day *Gujarat*, in the town of Somnath, which used to be called Bhairava, the arrow of a hunter killed Krisna.

History knows him by the name of Krisna or Vasudeva, his friends and peers knew him as Kanha, His devotees knew him as Ranachor Dev.

But *Anandamurtijii* has spoken about Krisna as Braj Krisna or Parthasarathi. Other names used for Krisna are - *Madhusudana* (slayer of the demon Madhu), Arisudana (slayer of enemies), Govinda (herdsman or giver of enlightenment), Vasudeva (son of *Vasudeva*), Yadava (descendant of Yadu), Keshava (having fine hair), Madhava (the husband of *Lakshmi*), Rsikesa (Lord of the senses, *Rsika, isa*), Janardana (the liberator of man).

Krisna lived for 85 years. His first seventeen years were spent in Gokul or Brindavan. From the age of 18 up to 65, Krisna was involved in conflict and battles, and in the end, he established *Dharmaraj*. From the age of 65 to 85 He taught *Yudhisthira* the art of running a kingdom in addition to showing him the path of a righteous life. This is how life was spent.

### Contribution of Lord Krishna

Shrii Krishna was not only a spiritual leader and a social reformer, but also an ideal politician, some pseudo-intellectuals of today raise lines on their foreheads if they hear of a spiritual leader speaking of politics. To cry down the link-up of politics with dharma has become a fashion of the day. A man, however might an exploiter, bourgeois or reactionary He might be, becomes progressive, nay a proletariat, if He dwells, at length, upon the so-called moral deprivation of a spiritual leader who propounds the doctrine that politics is not extraneous to *Dharma*. Shrii Krishna himself typified that it is tomfoolery. His diplomacy and rich counsel to Yudhisthir in regard to state administration exemplify his profound and practical knowledge of politics. It is therefore little wonder if the philosophers all over the world adjudge him as the best teacher and ideal politician not only of India but also of the world- (Shrii *Prabhat* ranjan Sarkar in His Abhimat, page-141)

He has shown that when moral codes of *Dharma* are associated with politics and when ideological control is superimposed to chasten the political activities, politics become healthy, a king enjoys his regeneration and a Chandashok (i.e. Ashok, the oppressor), turns out into a Dharmashok (i.e. Ashok the pious). Shrii Krishna teaches us that Dharma (i.e. universal religion that has nothing to do with dogmatic faith) is in no way inimical to politics.

The life that beams with the eternal light of intuition and

forges upward drawing sustenance from the soil of perseverance is bound to branch out into different channels of activity and glory itself with variety and richness. *Shrii Krsna* had a checkered life and an attempt may be risked to underline his characteristic features:

1. He had a balanced approach to knowledge, devotion and action.
2. He was up against priest crafts, which begot immorality.
3. He resuscitated the truth that man's life is meant for spiritual practice.
4. He got the moralist kings united against the immoral kings and brought about discomfiture of the evil forces.
5. He brought into light the psychological aspects of every human deed.
6. He did not send the white pigeons high up into the air in quest of peace; on the contrary, He taught men to blow the bugle and beat war-drums and wage a war against evil in a dispassionate but merciless frame of mind.
7. He was the epitome of protest against exploitation and immorality.
8. He synthesized eternal humanism with socio-political and spiritual activities.

*Shrii Krsna* was a chariot driver in the battlefield, but we do not fail to discover in him the greatest philosopher propounding the gospel of love and peace as contained in the *Giita*. He was the embodiment of terror to *Kansa*, *Shishupal* and *Jarasandha* but at the same time He was the apostle of peace in the court of *Duryodhana*. "He is unconquerable and unconquered, pure, holy, genial, kindhearted, enterprising, righteous, conversant with the *Vedas*, moral principles and spiritual cult, philanthropic, prone to pardoning, impartial, serene, merciless, and free from egotism, a great yogi, and monastic. He accomplishes His duties by virtue of human power, but his character is inhuman." (*Krsna Charitra*: *Bankim Chandra Chattopadhya*.)

### The Bhagvad Giita

Spiritual philosophy—The *Bhagavad Giita* is the essence of Indian *Vedic* wisdom and one of the great spiritual and philosophical classics of the world. It comes to us in the form of a battlefield dialogue between Lord *Krsna*, the supreme personality of Godhood, and *Arjuna*, his intimate friend and devotee, whom He instructs in the science of self-realization.

We do not know the name of the author of the *Bhagavad Giita*, almost all the really early books of literature of India are anonymous. The authorship of the Giita is attributed to *Vyasa*, the legendary compiler of the *Mahabharata*, the eighteen chapters of the *Giita* from chapter XXIII to XL of *Bhismaparva* of *Mahabharat*.

It is agreed that the great yogi, *Krsna*, could not have recited seven hundred verses to *Arjuna* on the battlefield. He must have said a few pointed things upon which were elaborated into an extensive work later by the narrator. The entire *Bhagavad Giita* is presented as a question-answer of Lord *Krsna* and *Arjuna*.

How the Story of the Giita Started?—In a vast field near *Hastinapur*, in the neighborhood of modern *Delhi*, *Dhrtarashtra*, the blind king of the *Kurus*, decided to give his throne to *Yudhisthiira*, who was known as *Dharmaraja*, the embodiment of virtue, in preference to his own eldest son, *Duryodhana*.

The latter, by tricks and treachery, secured the throne for him and attempted to destroy *Yudhisthiira* and his four brothers. *Krsna*, the head of Yadava clan, sought to bring about reconciliation between the cousins. When all attempts failed a fratricidal war between the *Kauravas* and the *Pandavas* became inevitable.

*Krsna* proposed that he and His vassals would join the two sides and left the choice to the two parties. *Duryodhana* selected the vassals and *Krsna* himself joined the *Pandavas* as the charioteer of *Arjuna*.

On the side of the Pandavas were the following great warriors:

Drstadyumna—the son of Drupada, the king of *Pancala*, commander in chief of *Pandavas*; Five Pandavas—Yudhisthiira, *Bhima*, Arjuna, *Nakul*, *Sahadeva*; *Yuyudhana*—Krsna's charioteer, also called *Satyaki*; Virata—the prince in whose state the *Pandavas* lived for some time in disguise; Dhrstaketu—the king of the Cedis; Cekitana—the famous warrior of the Pandavas; *Purujit* and *Kuntibhoja*—two brothers sometimes each taken for the other; *Saibya*—king of the Sibi tribe; Abhimanyu—the son of Arjuna; *Ghatotkaccha*—the son of *Bhima*; Krsna—the king of Dvarika.

The following were on the side of the *Kauravas*:

Bhisma—an old sage warrior who brought up Dhrtarashtra and *Pandu*; Karna—half brother to Arjuna; *Krpa*—the brother-in-law of Dronacarya; Dronacarya—the teacher of Kaurava and *Pandu*; Asvathama—the son of *Dronacarya*; Duryodhana, Dusasana and *Vikarna*—all Kauravas; *Somadatta*—the son of Somadatta, the king of the Bahlikas.

It is my opinion that the Kauravas' army was stronger than the *Pandava*. On the battlefield of Kurukshetra all the preparations for war were ready. That very morning, *Yudhisthiira*, the oldest brother of Pandavas, looks at the impenetrable formation organized by *Bhima*. Trembling with fear, he tells Arjuna, "How can victory be ours in the face of such an army?" Arjuna encourages his brother but he himself has a fear complex. Arjuna saw standing in the other army fathers and grandfathers, teachers, uncles, brothers, sons and grandsons, and also companions.

*Arjuna* had a strong belief, but other warriors on the *Pandava* side were not as strong as him. The teacher Drona, Krpacarya, Karna, Asvathama's grandfather Bhisma, they all had strong weapons in the battlefield. Lord Krsna had promised that there would be no need for weapons in the battlefield. In this connection, it is my opinion that Arjuna got his fear complex and no longer wanted to fight this war, when Lord *Krsna* tried to convince them through many arguments; those arguments came in the form of the Giita. According to the first chapter in verses 29, 30, Arjuna told *Krsna*, "My limbs quake, my mouth goes dry, my body shakes, and my hair stands on end. My bow, *Gandiva*, slips from my hand, and my skin is burning all over. I am unable to stand steady. My mind is reeling." This is not a symbolic sign of compassion or illusion; it is a sign of a fear complex.

### The Teaching of the Giita

1. There should be a balance in life between action, knowledge, and devotion.
2. The phenomenal world of matter and of individualized consciousness, the world of things, and animals and persons and even God, is the manifestation of divine ground within which all-partial realities have their being and apart from which they would be non-existent.
3. Human beings are capable not merely of knowing about the divine ground by inference; they can also realize its existence by a direct intuition superior to discursive reasoning. This immediate knowledge unites the knower with that which is known.
4. Man possesses a double nature—a phenomenal ego and an eternal self, which is the inner man, the spirit, and the spark of divinity within the soul. It is possible for a man, if he so desires, to identify himself with the spirit and therefore with the divine ground, which is the same or like nature with the spirit.
5. Man's (person's) life on the earth has only one end and purpose: to identify himself with his eternal self and so to knowledge of the Divine ground.
6. The yoga of the Giita is a progressive and multiple process. There is no loss in attempt and failure, as every

sincere effort is a gain in itself. (See chapter - 24, *Sadhana*)

The essence of the Giita - Why do you worry about any rhyme or reason? Who are you, Afraid of unavailingly? Who can kill you? Soul neither takes birth nor does it die.

Whatever happened, it happened for the good. What is happening is also for the good. Whatever will happen, that too will be for the good. Though do not repent over the past. Do not worry for the future. The present is going.

What have you lost for which you weep? What did you bring with you that you have lost? What had you produced, which has perished? You did not bring anything, whatever you have you have it from here. Whatever you have given you have given only here.

Whatever you have taken, it's from Him, the almighty. Whatever you rendered, it was rendered to Him. You came empty handed and you will go the same way. Whatever is yours today, it was somebody else's yesterday, and will be somebody else, day after tomorrow. You are delighted in feeling it is your own. Alas, this happiness is the root cause of your agonies. Change is the law of the universe. What you understand as death, is reality in life. You become a millionaire in moments. and in the next you become a pauper. Let the thoughts about mine-yours, small-big, my own-someone else's be wiped off your mind and thought. Then everything is yours and you belong to everyone.

Neither this body is yours, nor do you belong to the body. The body is constituted of fire, water, air, earth, and ethereal fluid and it will disappear into these.

However steady the soul, then so you are. You surrender yourself to God, which is that best anchor. One who knows about this anchor is liberated from fear, anxiety and sorrow forever.

Whatever you act upon, continue to dedicate it to God. By so doing, you will realize the happiness of a liberated one.

## My Master Speaks About Lord Krsna

Lord *Krsna's* personality has two distinct faces—one is *Krsna* of *Vraja*, king of *Mathura*. He had to play two different roles because of the differences in the hinterland of his duty. People in general were not as easily intimate or familiar with emperor *Krsna* (*Parthasarathi Krsna*) - as they were with the Krsna of Vrindavana.

*Krsna* of *Vraja* was a sweet personality and the sweetness was mixed with spirituality, whereas *Krsna* the king was a tough personality but His toughness was also mixed with spirituality. In both roles, *Krsna* set a unique example before the Indian people as well as the people of the world. The need to hold that example before others is not yet over. The first part of *Krsna's* life is that of *Vraja's Krsna* yet here, I propose to take up another aspect of His personality—*Krishna* the King of *Parthasarathi*.

Let us now come to the context of **Krsna the King:**

He came from *Mathura* after leaving *Gokula* and *Vrindavana*. Velour, intrepidity, and all such good virtues were very evident in *Krsna* during His early career, in the *Krsna* of *Gokula* and *Vrindavana*, but the element of sweetness was the most predominant quality in Him. To attract and draw the people closer to him by playing sweet and melodious tunes on His flute, to establish the sweet relations of love and affection with all, and in case of necessity, to take up arms in the interests of His favorite friends and followers these were the types of deeds He used to do. The *Gopiis* felt that Krshna was theirs. They would say, He is one of us but a far greater hero, as far superior to us in all aspects. but He is still ours". But the person who did very much for His devotees and remained steadfastly amongst them in prosperity and diversity, the man found that it was impossible to do the maximum good for humanity in that role because humanity was unbearably persecuted, humiliated and neglected. Therefore, he gave up that role and assumed the role of a king, and that role started with the

annihilation of *Kansa*.

Meaning of **Kansa**? It means an entity that is dangerous for others' existences, which is an impediment in the path of others' welfare, others' all-round progress. On the other hand, Krsna means an entity that leads others to fulfillment, towards the perfect attainment—one who cannot tolerate destructive ideas or destructive elements. Krsna was destined to annihilate *Kansa*. He removes the thorns of sin from the physical, psychic, and spiritual planes, and this is what *Dharma* is.

Those forces that are the main obstacles in the path of social progress must be removed. Mercy or compassion may intervene to suggest, "No, don't do that," but circumstances complete it, and for this element toughness is indispensable. This was not possible in the sweet and loving atmosphere of *Vrajabhumi*. The environment of *Kuruksetra* was proper for the purpose.

First, we shall analyze how the word *Partha* came. The word *Partha* is derived from *Partha* to *Prthvi*. *Partha* is the name of a particular kingdom and *Kunti* was the name of the princess of that kingdom. The word *Partha* is derived from the word *Parth* + suffix *sna*, and is used to denote the source or origin. That is, *Partha* means a son of *Kunti* or *Kaonteya*.

In ancient India, before the arrival of the Aryans, the matriarchal system of inheritance as well as the matrilineal order of the society was in vogue among the Austric and Dravidian communities, that is, while the people of those prehistoric times used to identify themselves in terms of their mother's lineage, they used to take the name of their mothers. If someone asked, "Who are you?" the person had to say his or her mother's name. Prior to that, in still more ancient days, the people used to identify themselves as the grandchildren of their mother's mother or father's mother. The matrilineal order of the society was in vogue. Along with it went the matriarchal system of social inheritance whereby the daughters would inherit the property from their mothers. Even two thousand years ago while naming a child, the mother's name was considered the most important factor. For instance, Lord *Buddha*'s two prominent disciples were *Sariputra* and *Mahamoggallana Arahan*. *Sariputta* means the son of *Sari* and his mother's full name was *Rupasari* (they were born in *Magadha*). Because the mother's name was *Rupasri*, the son's name became *Sariputra* in *Samskrta*. In Prakrit, it was changed to *Rajaputta*. In the other's case, the mother's name was *Maha Modgalii* in *Samskrta* and in *Pali* it became *Mahamoggali*. Her son became known as *Mahamoggallan Arahan*.

Here is another example: a merchant promised *Buddha* that he would build a new city, the capital of *Magadha*, near the confluence of the *Ganga* and some other rivers. The name of the merchant was *Pataliputra* because his mother's name was *Patali*. This demonstrates that ancient Indian society followed the matrilineal order. *Arjuna* was also popularly known as *Partha* because he was the son of *Kunti*. As already said, *Krsna*'s major role was Parthasarathi. Now let us explain *Sarathi*. *Sarathi* means one who looks upon a chariot as his own child. You will come across some car drivers, maybe few in number, who take meticulous care and trouble for their motor cars. They take very special care to see that their cars are not touched or damaged in any way. In fact, they treat their cars as if they were their own children. Similarly, one who looks upon the *ratha* or chariot as one's own child is called *Sarathi*. *Rathena sah ityarthe sarathi* + suffix (in the sense of child) *Sarathi*. The Sarathi or Charioteer of *Partha* Arjuna was Krsna Himself. Krsna assumed the role of *Parthasarathi*.

In ancient times, human beings were not so much developed compared to the animals. There was no difference between primitive humans and proto-apes living in caves. But one branch of primitive humans made significant advances in knowledge, in intellect, whereas other branches lagged far behind. The normal speed of the other groups failed to keep pace with the accelerated speed of the former so they continued to trail behind. Apes could

not rise above the animal's stage whereas humans felt that they were something new, something different, from other species.

While advancing, humans learned how to build houses, but still they retained some animal traits. For example, animals and monkeys would dance, the peacock would dance, and those primitive humans would also dance. However, whereas the animals did not know how to cook meals and hence took raw food, humans gradually learned how to cook and roast food. It was Lord *Shiva* who trained those primitive humans, who were neither fully humans nor completely animal to live a harmonious and systematic life. Since those people would dance, Shiva taught them the art of dancing. He introduced a fixed system in every sphere of life. *Shiva* also noticed that the people of His age wanted to think, so to contemplate, so He gave a particular line to think along which would help them in their psychic progress and spiritual elevation. He introduced rhythm, a systematic lifestyle, into the lives of primitive people and showed the path that leads towards subtlety. This highest and greatest contribution of Lord *Shiva* was that He helped develop each and every aspect of human life from the animal stage and brought them within the domain of subtlety. That tradition has continued for many thousands of years. However, that was not enough for human society. Society has not yet realized the need or significance of collective living. It was an imperative necessity for people to feel, to realize that there was the utmost need of a society guaranteeing the basic needs of food, clothing, shelter, education, and medical treatment for all its members. People need to understand that, just as they could carry on the daily routine of their individual lives, others could also have the opportunity to follow the same broad highway of morality and spirituality in collective life. Otherwise, the degeneration of the individual is inevitable.

Only a handful of people, a small community of people comprises of the small kingdom of Aunga, *Vaunga*, Kalinga, Saorastra, *Magatha* etc. If a problem of unprecedented magnitude ever arose before them, they were not able to grapple with it individually; hence they had to face it unitedly. Teaching society this lesson was the onerous responsibility that fell upon Krsna and to bring home this point to His contemporaries, He had to assume the role of *Parthsarathi*.

This was a prodigious task, beset with difficulties and obstacles. Contemporary society, or for that matter ancient India, was fragmented into a large number of small kingdoms. Krsna fervently hoped that all the petty kingdoms would unite together and construct a well-knit society, a stable, greater India. In that strong and united India, working in coordinated cooperation among the federated units, socio-economic life would be well based and stable, the moral standard would improve, and consequently people would progress spiritually. This mission extended from individuals to the collectivity. Such was the aspiration of Krsna. Simultaneously, if we analyze the role of Krsna of *Braja*, we notice that He was guiding the people along the path of devotion. He was the very embodiment of softness and sweetness. He did not show the severity needed in battle. Of course, struggle was present but the primary factor was devotion. To guide people along the devotional path was the main theme and the realization they received was Aparoksa *Anubhuti* or direct experience. But the realization about *Parasarathi Krsna* was both direct and indirect. For instance a person develops a superior intellect. They build a happy prosperous society and cultivate higher knowledge, thereby determining the dos and don'ts of social life. They learned the work through the cultivation of the science of Yoga as well as by learning the practical processes of how to finish any work deftly, adroitly. These lessons fall within the scope of indirect realization or Paroksa Anubhuti. Parthasarathi showed humanity the path of knowledge, which is in itself an invaluable aid to Aparoksa Anubhuti. Likewise, He demonstrated the art of *Karma* Yoga which is in it Paroksa anubhuti but which is a most valuable aid to *Aparoksa Anubhuti*.

He further taught *sharanagati*, the cult of surrender, that is, how to surrender to *Parama Purusa* and come to His supreme shelter. This is the stage where *Paroksa* and *Aparoksa Anubhuti* becomes one.

On the other hand, *Krsna* of *Vraja* showed humanity the way to attain *Aparoksa anubhuti*. The humans, the *Jiivas*, came closest to *Parama Purusa* through devotion, through sweet love for him, a state that is otherwise known as *Radha Bhava*. There Krsna taught His devotees that they must feel the world, for everything in this world is sweet, people have to taste the sweetness through severity, harshness and struggle in order to experience the taste of the Supreme sweetness. Struggle in life is inevitable. This is the foremost difference between the two roles of Krsna. One wants to take humanity to highest realization through *Aparoksa Anubhuti*, while the other led humanity to *Aparoksa Anubhuti* through *Paroksa Anubhuti*, and then finally the highest state of human excellence.

The difference is very vast indeed. The resoluteness, the firmness which is found in *Parthasarathi Krsna* is conspicuously absent in *Krsna* of *Vraja*. Similarly, virtues are found in *Krsna* of *Braja*, are totally absent in *Parthasarathi*. *Parthasarathi Krsna* is ever ready to take up arms for the protection for those who have come under His shelter, whereas Krsna of *Vraja* protected His followers with softness and charming sweetness. He never exhibited His toughness, His firmness to anyone.

Can anyone say who is the great amongst two? My reply is this. The question of who is superior or inferior of the two does not arise at all. The question is irrelevant. The fact is *Krsna* had to assume different roles because of necessity. In one role He converted the ordinary community of people into a God-loving and highly devotional groups and in another role; he goaded that same community to greater and nobler deeds, and established them in the highest and excellence of human glory. Both were equally necessary and both are necessary even today.

# IX

## MY MASTER: HIS CONTRIBUTION

A universal ideology, which is not confined to time, space, and person but based on the Absolute, is necessary to unite humanity. When the limited mind accepts the unlimited entity as its objects, the mind goes on expanding to a full 360 degrees. The method that brings about psycho-spiritual progress is called spiritual practice. When human beings bring the entire universe within the range of their minds through spiritual practice, the result will be one universal society.

### His Allround Contribution

(My master, *Prabhat Rainjan Sarkar* is one of the truly remarkable people of our time. His contributions to modern thought have been both brilliant and wide ranging. Much of His work has taken place in the world).

My master on Economics—"I want every human being to be guaranteed the minimum physical requirements of life, and to get scope for the full development of his or her potentialities. Every human being must get an equal opportunity to attain the truth, this never ending effort of proper economic adjustment must ceaselessly continue at all times with a view to assisting the spiritual, mental and physical evolution of humanity."

My Master, *P.R. Sarkar* is not simply an economist solely concerned with productivity and trade issues, or traditional economic problems. Rather, He rethinks the underlying assumptions of economics in order to transform public policies and create a new society.

To begin with, He expands economic thought and recategorizes it into four parts. He calls these people's economy, psycho-economy, the commercial economy and the general economy.

People's Economy—This part of the economy will deal with the essential needs of the people in general. Their production, distribution, marketing, shopping, storage, pricing, sales, freight, charges, preformat costing, and all related activities. Most importantly, it is directly concerned with the guaranteed provision of minimum requirements like food, clothes, housing, medical services, education, agriculture, and the proper use of irrigation waters. Continuous improvement and

ready availability of these essentials is a key factor in the people's economy.

Minimum requirements must be assured through guaranteed purchasing power, which should be enshrined in the constitution as a fundamental human right, or as a cardinal human right, this will give the citizens of a country power to sue a government if their minimum requirements are not met. Hence the necessity of the people's economy will be reinforced by constitutional power.

"As people's economy will deal with minimum requirements and people's subsistence problems, it must take precedence over other parts of the economy. For example, if people have no food and are starving, it may be necessary to establish short-term uneconomic industries to supply food. In normal conditions, such industries would violate the logic and demands of general economic principles and principles of supply and demand. The People's economy should also be concerned with the development of small-scale industries, both private and cooperative. Private industries would be limited in size and scope to prevent monopoly production and exploitation, and would be required to function as cooperatives once they grew too large. Cooperative industries are the best means of organizing people in an independent manner so that they take collective responsibility for their livelihood.

The people's economy may deal with employment of all, the eradication of mass poverty, rural economy, the phase-wise socialization of land into the hands of those who work physically or intellectually for proper production, practical training programs to impart specific skills to enable people to find employment in their immediate urban or rural locality, work placement and transportation, transshipment and loading/unloading of any materials, even if they are not economically viable. Another important aspect of the people's economy may also be concerned with power, water, and energy supply, as small scale and cheap power and water production are essential if people are to have complete control over their economic lives. Finally, the people's economy may further deal with economic decentralization, people's power and cooperative dynamo, and block-level planning."

Psycho Economy—The psycho economy is concerned with increasing the psychic pabulum (perspective, purposes) of the individual and the group through appropriate economic activity.

"While the people's economy will be the main concern of poor and underdeveloped economic units, the psycho economy will gain increasing importance once the problems of subsistence are gradually solved. Thus, the psycho-economy would be of major importance in a highly developed and mechanized economy where people may only work for a few hours a week and have a lot of spare time. Psycho-economy will have two branches. The first one will endeavor to eradicate exploitative and unjust economic practices, behaviors, and structures. It will counter all economic and psycho-economic exploitation and make people more aware of how capitalists in their singular or collective roles exploit society through their gigantic propaganda machinery, creating unhealthy artificial demands, which may not only poison the mind but also encourage dangerous habits detrimental to psychic sanity and expansion. The first and foremost duty of psycho-economics is to wage a tireless war against degenerating and dehumanizing economic trends in society.

The second branch of psycho-economics is to develop and enhance the psychic pabulum of the individual and collective minds. This branch is virtually unknown to economists today, but it will become an extremely important branch of economics in the future when mundane economic problems have been developed. Psycho-economics will add to the glaring glamour of economics; Psycho-economics will find creative solutions to economic problems to nurture the maximum utilization of

psychic and spiritual potentialities in creation.

Psycho-economy includes the growth of a balanced economy, the selection of production items, allocation decisions, the creation of proper work environments, the application of east-west theory, full employment policies, workers participation and control over production and distribution, economic independence for women, incentive policies, psychological factors in progressive rationalization, psychological and sociological dimensions of economic development, the implementation of technology to increase production and reduce working hours, quality of life issues, business ethics and moral and ethical principles and practices for effective cooperative management."

**Commercial Economy**— This branch of economics, according to Shrii P.R. Sarkar, is to be concerned with questions of productivity. Commercial economy is similar to the present understanding of economics.

"This part of the economy will develop scientific, efficient methods of production which will not incur loss, and where output will exceed input. The aim of commercial economy is maximum utilization and rational distribution of the resources of the universe. It includes the selection, production and distribution of cash crops, inter-zonal and intra-zonal trade policies, export-import policies, rationalization or organized marketing systems, fixation of profit rates, cost accounting, licensing policies, technology transfer policies, technical quality control, management of balance and trade and payments, capital formation and loan policies, government intervention, monetary and fiscal policy, banking, capital supply, the barter system, the international monetary system, the organization of commerce, and the planning of both agrico and agro industries and non agricultural industries."

**General Economy** - This aspect of the economy and economics is concerned with the history of economic thought and a comparison of the various economic theories—among others, capitalist, socialist and PROUTIST.

"PROUT is based on a three-tiered economic structure, large-scale key industries managed as public utilities, the cooperative economic sector, the largest sector in a Proutist economy, and small scale concerns owned privately as partnerships or corporations. Key industries will function on a no loss, no profit principles.

General economy may also include economic infrastructure, coordination of economic planning at all levels, block-level planning, future planning, population policy, employment policy, full-employment policy, agro-industrial policies, socialization of the economy, development of cooperative organizations, the banking system how to increase purchasing capacity, macro and micro economic problems, tax structure, urban and rural development, decentralization, classification of the three-tiered industrial structure, defense and planning and expenditure, national or zonal planning, and the application of appropriate technology and economic progress."

**Capitalism and Communism**— While the previous section developed the four main parts of the economy, this section will attempt to critique the capitalist and communist discourses, the theories, the practices, and texts that constitute these two monumental and dominant economic world views of the past few centuries.

My Master first explains the ideas behind capitalism.

"The impetus from crude psychic urges gave birth to Capitalism. In capitalism, the psychology of the acquisition of material wealth, be it land, money, metal, or other property, strongly predominates. Such crude psychic urges and psychic pabulum remain unchecked and unbridled in capitalism and turn into a hungry profit motive in the market system. As a result, traders, industrialists, and business people suffer from the psychic disease of accumulating more and more wealth by any

means, even to the point of depriving other human beings of their basic requirements.

Those dominated by these hungry psychic urges or psychic pabulum run after material gains and do not hesitate to exploit others mercilessly. Exploitation starts at the point where one violates the principle of *Aparigraha* (non-indulgence in those amenities and comforts, which are superfluous for physical existence) and accumulates more physical wealth than one actually needs for survival and progress in the world. The exploiters forget the basic truth that this material world is very limited, whereas psychic pabula are propelled by an unlimited urge. When unlimited pabula are let loose in the unlimited world, exploitation starts. A few became rich and other became poor. In such a condition, millions die without food, live without shelter, work without education and splits into two distinct groups—the haves and the have-nots. The former is the class of the exploiters—the capitalists—and the latter the class of exploited—the disgruntled workers. So the unchecked psychic urges and the psychic pabula for material acquisition end in merciless exploitation. This inhuman exploitation causes the mass-level deprivation of millions of people. The curses of capitalism engulf the whole society. Thus capitalism is anti-human."

However, my master does not support **Communism**.

"Communism is also a socio-economic political theory based on materialism. In the communist society, people's psychic urges and psychic pabulum instinctively run after material acquisition and crude enjoyment. When psychic pabulum is guided by a material outlook, human beings develop negative behavior pattern. For example, they become extroversial in nature, develop strong attachment to material wealth and worldly pleasures, become aggressively intolerant of other's views and thoughts, resort to brute force to repress opponents, deny the existence of spirituality and suppress the psychic urges or pabulum of the people. Common society suffers from all these evils.

In such a defective society, life loses its dynamism, the power of imagination gets shadowed and the urge for initiative weakened. Thus the communist society sinks into a state of doldrums. This degradation quickens the downfall of communism. Today, communist society is heading towards the inevitable tragic end."

From my master's perspective, both capitalism and communism are not the solutions to the global crises facing humanity today, rather they are the problem.

"Both capitalism and communism are anti-human. Under both these systems, psychic urges and pabula, instead of being properly channelized, are involved in detrimental physical, psycho physical, and psychic interactions, causing *Pratisamcara* or negative movement in the mind.

In capitalism, the rich, in their affluence, misuse their psychic urge and direct their psychic pabula towards the pursuit of material gains. And the poor, in their extreme poverty, misguide their psychic urges and concentrate their psychic pabula and anti-social activities in their struggle for existence. In communism, the elite and the party leaders misuse their psychic urges and direct their psychic pabula towards political manipulation and totalitarian repression. Under the oppression of communist rule, the psychic pabula of the general people are suppressed into a state of doldrums. In both cases, psychic urges are misutilized for sub-human activities in the physical, psycho physical and psychic spheres".

General Principles of PROUT - Sarkar's reconstitution of economy and economics, as well as His critique of both capitalism and communism, what then are the principles and values that inform His preferred system, PROUT. First, we must define ownership:

While capitalism rests ownership with the individual, and communism with the state, my master does not believe that

property can in fact be owned at all.

"We should not forget even for a moment that this whole animate world is a large family in which nature has not assigned any property to any particular individual. Selfish opportunists have created individual ownership so that they might take advantage of the defects of this system in order to grow fatter in a parasitic way. When the whole property of this universe has been inherited by all creatures, how then can there be any justification for a system in which someone receives a huge excess, while others die for lack of a handful of grain?"

However, my master does not advocate the abolishment of personal property. It is the hoarding of wealth that is the problem. Thus, He believes that localities, regions, and federations must establish ceilings and floors.

"The universe is the common property of all. All people have right of enjoyment, but no one has the right to misuse this common property. Those who accumulate much wealth and hoard it directly curtail the happiness and convenience of others in the society. Their behavior is flagrantly anti-social. Therefore, no one should be allowed to hoard wealth without the permission of society."

For *Sarkar*, the different regions should establish various ceilings of property use, but there should never be any limits to psychic and spiritual development, intellectual liberty, spiritual freedom and the right to free movement throughout the world cannot be denied for labor.

"In this socio-economic setup, human beings are at full liberty in the spiritual and mental spheres. This is possible because the spiritual and psychic entities for which people can aspire are themselves unlimited, and the extent of possession in this sphere does not hamper the progress of others in their quests. But supply in the physical sphere is limited, and hence any effort for disproportionate or unrestricted acquiring of physical objects has every possibility of creating a vast majority of have nots and thus hampering the spiritual, mental, and physical growth of the larger majority. So while dealing with the problem of individual liberty in the physical sphere, liberty must not be allowed to cross a limit where it is instrumental in hampering the development of the complete personality of human beings."

"Let everyone enjoy as much as they like the subtle mental plane. Let everyone possess as much mental and spiritual wealth as possible. Really speaking, both physical and mental cravings are born of mental hunger. It is certainly possible to transform the craving for physical enjoyment into intellectual hunger by channeling it toward a different goal through proper education. Human society today is badly in need of such education."

PROUT regulates the accumulated wealth in certain boundaries and it legislates basic necessities.

"The availability of minimum essentialities of life plays a vital part not only in achieving world fraternity but also in the development of the human personality. This should be tackled on a world footing and should be based on certain fundamental presumptions. Every human being has certain minimum requirements which must be guaranteed him or her. Guaranteed availability of foodstuffs, clothing, medical assistance, and housing accommodation should be arranged so that people may be able to utilize their surplus energy (energy up with now engaged in procuring the essentials of life) in subtler pursuits. Side by side, there should be sufficient scope for providing other amenities of the progressive age. To fulfill the above responsibilities, enough purchasing capacity should be created."

At the same time, however, my master believes there is a need for a dynamic and incentive structure.

"If the supply of requirements be guaranteed without any conditions of personal skill and labor, the individual may develop a psychology of idleness. The minimum requirements of every person are the same, but diversity is also the nature of

creation. Special amenities should therefore be provided so that the diversity skill and intelligence is fully utilized, and talent is encouraged to contribute to its best for human development. It will, therefore, be necessary to make provision for special emolument which can cater to special amenities of life according to the age and time. But at the same time, there should be a constant effort to reduce the gap between the amount of special emolument and the bare minimum requirements must be liberalized by increasing the provision of special amenities pertaining to the age, and also simultaneously bringing about a decrease in the provision of special emolument given to a few."

**Economic Balance** - So far, I have presented *P.R. Sarkar's* four-fold division of economics, His critique of capitalism and communism, as well as the basic principles of PROUT. I now turn to perhaps the key problem in the world economy today, that of disparity between rich and poor, within nations and between nations. *Sarkar's* model does not put the first World as directly related to the poverty of the Third World. Moreover, progress for Him can only be defined in terms of intellectual and spiritual development, not goods and services consumed. For Him, the key concept is balance or Prama.

"Nature has been beautiful to each and every region of the world and has endowed enormous wealth both on and under the surface of the earth. In fact, different areas of the world are full of agricultural, mineral, aquatic, medicinal, and forest resource, yet in many economic regions of the world there abound utmost poverty, low standard of life, and cultural and industrial backwardness. As a result, carrying the curse of acute scarcity of food, clothes, and accommodations, as well as a lack of educational facilities, millions of people today in the 20th century, when material science claims to have made strident progress, are now fighting for their physical survival. There is no shortage of physical wealth in any economic region, because of blessings of nature. But due to the lack of benevolent propensities, those materials cannot be utilized for social and economic development. Consequently, the basic physical necessities of people (food, clothes, accommodation, and medical treatment) could not be fulfilled. So there is eventually large scale want of '*Prama*' in the physical sphere."

The world as we know it today is rooted in injustice. Capitalism and communism are theories that serve to legitimize these injustices. *Sarkar* graphically describes these conditions.

"What we see in the market place and railway stations is the exhibition for half-clad street beggars and lepers all stretching out their begging bowls, the only means of earning their livelihood. They are fortunate if anybody contemptuously flings a copper coin at them. The old blind beggars wait all day long at the bridge and lift their bowls as soon as some one walks pass them. Their shrill cries fall on deaf ears only. On the other hand, the affluence of food kept ready in luxurious houses to entertain the big guns of society ridicules the present civilization."

"People of a particular country die of starvation, and people of another country die of overeating and indigestion. This disparity is a creation of selfish men, not of nature, not of the supreme consciousness. All are equal, with equal glamour as human beings."

For the materially poor of the world in particular, the injustice is cleverly camouflaged.
